[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I’ve started having thoughts that really scared me. It’s not something I’m proud of saying out loud, but I keep catching myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And sometimes, especially on the drive home, my mind goes to this place where I wonder if my family, or even my students, would somehow be better off without me around.\n\nI don’t want to act on it — I really don’t — and that’s part of why I came in, because the fact that those thoughts are showing up at all is frightening. I’m a teacher, I’m used to being responsible and holding things together for other people, and lately these thoughts have felt… too heavy to just keep brushing aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these thoughts about not wanting to wake up or wondering if others would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I’d like to admit. About a year and seven months, I think. It started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nAt first I don’t think I even recognized them as those kinds of thoughts. It was more like, “I’m so exhausted, I wish I could just disappear for a while,” or, “I don’t want to wake up and have to do all of this again.” But over time it became more direct and harder to ignore. And because that project just kept stretching on, and I still had my regular classes and responsibilities, it feels like those thoughts have been kind of… following me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong, because I used to be the kind of person who had things I genuinely looked forward to.\n\nI used to play softball, and even if I was tired from school, once I got there I’d usually feel better — being outside, joking with people, just focusing on the game for a while. Now I either cancel or I show up and it’s like I’m physically there but not really… enjoying any of it. I’m just counting down until I can go home.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love reading and talking about the book, even when we got completely off topic. Lately I either haven’t finished the book or I go and just sit there, nodding, trying to seem normal. My garden too — that used to be calming for me, checking on things after work, planting, weeding, all of that. Now it feels like another chore I’m failing at.\n\nAnd robotics club at school… that one makes me feel especially guilty, because the students are excited and I know it matters to them. I still try to be there, but I don’t feel that spark I used to feel. I’m mostly going through the motions. It’s like the things that used to make me feel like myself don’t really reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience pleasure when you're doing these activities, even if you're not looking forward to them as much?\n\nPatient: Not really… no. I keep hoping that maybe if I just get myself there, the feeling will come back once I’m in it, because that used to happen. Like with softball, even if I’d had a long day, once we started playing I could usually get caught up in it and laugh or feel competitive or just enjoy being outside. Now I can be standing there on the field and it’s like I’m watching myself participate. I know it’s something I used to love, but I don’t actually feel much from it.\n\nBook club is similar. I’ll go sometimes because I don’t want people to notice I’m pulling away, but I’m mostly just sitting there, trying to follow along and seem interested. The conversation that used to feel fun just feels like noise I have to respond to.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get this quiet satisfaction from it, like checking on the plants after work felt grounding. Now even when I do it, it doesn’t feel peaceful. It just feels like another thing I’m supposed to care about.\n\nRobotics club too. I still care about the students, of course, and I know it matters, but the enjoyment part — the excitement I used to have — it’s just not really there. So I guess the answer is no, not much pleasure. Mostly I’m either canceling or going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you're moving, like feeling slowed down or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is really low. It’s not just being tired at the end of a normal workday, though I keep trying to tell myself that’s all it is. It feels heavier than that.\n\nLike, by the time I’ve gotten through teaching all day and then sat through a department meeting, my body feels so heavy. Even things that shouldn’t be a big deal — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, getting my bag ready for the next day — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “just for a minute” and then it’s like I can’t make myself get back up.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I’m restless exactly. It’s more slowed down, I guess. Like everything takes more effort than it should, and I’m constantly trying to push through this thick layer of exhaustion just to do basic things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like pants that used to fit normally are kind of hanging on me now, and I’ve had to tighten belts or just avoid certain things because they look strange.\n\nI’m not trying to lose weight. It’s more that on school days especially, I’ll realize it’s late afternoon and I haven’t really eaten anything. Sometimes I forget, or I know I should eat but I just don’t want to deal with it, so I put it off. Then by the time I notice, it’s like the whole day has gone by. So… yes, there’s been weight loss, or at least enough that I can see it in how my clothes fit, but it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had stressful stretches before — teaching kind of has seasons where everything feels like too much, and I’ve had times where I was exhausted or discouraged or just not myself for a bit. But I don’t know if I’d say it was like this.\n\nI’m trying to think back and not exaggerate or minimize it. There were times in college where I got overwhelmed, and other times during particularly rough school years where I’d sort of shut down for a weekend or feel low for a while. But I can’t clearly point to another period and say, yes, this was the same thing happening before. This feels… different, or at least more persistent, but I don’t know if that’s just because I’m in it right now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Not in a way I can confidently identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I’ve had times where I felt low before, or like I wasn’t really myself, but I’m not sure I’d describe them as the same kind of disconnected feeling.\n\nLike, there have been stressful periods where I wasn’t enjoying things much because I was overwhelmed or exhausted, but I can’t tell if that counts as a separate “period” in the way you mean. I’m trying not to over-answer or make it sound more definite than it is. This time feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s lasted and seeped into things, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t think I can confidently say yes, more than one period. I’m honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low and disconnected before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know I’ve said that a few times, but that really is the most honest answer I can give.\n\nI can remember feeling unhappy or overwhelmed before, definitely. I mean, I don’t think anyone gets to 33 without having some stretches where things feel heavy or where you’re sort of going through the motions. And with teaching, there have been periods where I felt worn down and not very connected to much outside of just getting through the week.\n\nBut when you say “this low and disconnected,” I hesitate, because I don’t know if I’ve had this exact thing before. I can’t point to another time in my life and say, yes, that was just like this. Maybe there were smaller versions of it, or times that looked similar from the outside, but I don’t know. This feels harder to shake, I guess, or more like it’s become part of the background.\n\nSo… maybe not? Or at least not in a way that I can clearly identify. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Is this the first time in your life you've felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I’ve had low times before, like I said, but this is the first time I can remember it affecting the things I usually actually look forward to in this way.\n\nLike softball — I used to really enjoy that. Even if I was tired after school, I’d usually feel better once I got there. Same with book club, or working in my garden, or helping with the robotics club. Those were things that felt like mine, or at least things that gave the week some shape.\n\nNow it’s more like I either cancel, or I show up and I’m just sort of… physically there. I go through the motions. I’ll sit at book club and listen, or I’ll go out to the garden and stare at what needs doing, but none of it really feels enjoyable. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something from them isn’t really connecting.\n\nSo if you mean this specific kind of low and disconnected from things I normally enjoy, then yes, I think this is the first time it’s felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before that resolved on its own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have a clear example of that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad and then eventually felt better, but I’m not sure I’d call those an “episode like this.” And I haven’t really had treatment for something like this before, at least not in a way where I could say, “Oh, that happened, and then it resolved because of treatment.”\n\nIt’s hard because I can look back and think, maybe there were periods where I was struggling more than I admitted at the time, and maybe I just pushed through because work kept going and life kept going. But I can’t point to a specific stretch that was this same kind of low and disconnected, and then say it clearly lifted on its own or with help.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is no, not that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low and disconnected in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not more than one in the way you’re asking.\n\nI keep turning it over because I don’t want to accidentally make it sound more certain than it is, but if I’m being honest, I can’t identify another separate time in my life where I felt this same low and this disconnected from things. I’ve had rough periods, definitely, and times where I was stressed or exhausted or not especially happy, but this feels different enough that I wouldn’t count those as the same thing.\n\nSo I’d say… no, not more than one episode that I can clearly point to. This is the only one I recognize as feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least, not in the way I used to.\n\nI keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to say “nothing” if that’s not completely fair, but the main things I used to actually look forward to just don’t feel good anymore. Softball used to be something I’d be excited about, even if I was tired — once I got there, I’d usually get into it. Now I either cancel or I go and I’m just kind of there, doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSame with book club. I used to like reading the book and hearing what people thought, even if I didn’t agree with them. Now I’ll sit there and listen, but it feels like I’m watching it happen from a distance. My garden is like that too. I know what needs to be done, and normally I’d enjoy being outside and getting my hands in the soil, but now I mostly just stare at it or make myself do little bits without really feeling anything from it.\n\nAnd robotics club — I used to enjoy helping the students problem-solve and seeing them get excited, but lately it feels more like another thing on the list. I don’t hate it. That’s the strange part. It’s not like I suddenly dislike all these things. It’s just that they don’t reach me the same way. So I guess I mostly go through the motions, or I avoid them, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to sort of compare it to other times I felt low, because that seems like the natural thing to do, but I can’t quite make the pieces fit.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think this is the first time it’s been like this.

I’m hesitating because I don’t want to make it sound too neat, like there’s a clear line in my life where everything before was fine and now this is the only bad period I’ve ever had. That’s not true. I’ve had times when I felt low before, or worn out, or unhappy with how things were going. But when I think about whether I’ve had something similar to this specific feeling — this sort of low, disconnected, dulled-out feeling — I can’t really point to another time and say, “Yes, that was the same.”

So… as far as I can tell, yes, this is the first time I’ve experienced it like this. There may have been rough patches before, but not something I’d recognize as the same kind of thing.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I’ve started having thoughts that really scared me. It’s not something I’m proud of saying out loud, but I keep catching myself thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up. And sometimes, especially on the drive home, my mind goes to this place where I wonder if my family, or even my students, would somehow be better off without me around.\n\nI don’t want to act on it — I really don’t — and that’s part of why I came in, because the fact that those thoughts are showing up at all is frightening. I’m a teacher, I’m used to being responsible and holding things together for other people, and lately these thoughts have felt… too heavy to just keep brushing aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these thoughts about not wanting to wake up or wondering if others would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I’d like to admit. About a year and seven months, I think. It started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nAt first I don’t think I even recognized them as those kinds of thoughts. It was more like, “I’m so exhausted, I wish I could just disappear for a while,” or, “I don’t want to wake up and have to do all of this again.” But over time it became more direct and harder to ignore. And because that project just kept stretching on, and I still had my regular classes and responsibilities, it feels like those thoughts have been kind of… following me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong, because I used to be the kind of person who had things I genuinely looked forward to.\n\nI used to play softball, and even if I was tired from school, once I got there I’d usually feel better — being outside, joking with people, just focusing on the game for a while. Now I either cancel or I show up and it’s like I’m physically there but not really… enjoying any of it. I’m just counting down until I can go home.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love reading and talking about the book, even when we got completely off topic. Lately I either haven’t finished the book or I go and just sit there, nodding, trying to seem normal. My garden too — that used to be calming for me, checking on things after work, planting, weeding, all of that. Now it feels like another chore I’m failing at.\n\nAnd robotics club at school… that one makes me feel especially guilty, because the students are excited and I know it matters to them. I still try to be there, but I don’t feel that spark I used to feel. I’m mostly going through the motions. It’s like the things that used to make me feel like myself don’t really reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience pleasure when you're doing these activities, even if you're not looking forward to them as much?\n\nPatient: Not really… no. I keep hoping that maybe if I just get myself there, the feeling will come back once I’m in it, because that used to happen. Like with softball, even if I’d had a long day, once we started playing I could usually get caught up in it and laugh or feel competitive or just enjoy being outside. Now I can be standing there on the field and it’s like I’m watching myself participate. I know it’s something I used to love, but I don’t actually feel much from it.\n\nBook club is similar. I’ll go sometimes because I don’t want people to notice I’m pulling away, but I’m mostly just sitting there, trying to follow along and seem interested. The conversation that used to feel fun just feels like noise I have to respond to.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get this quiet satisfaction from it, like checking on the plants after work felt grounding. Now even when I do it, it doesn’t feel peaceful. It just feels like another thing I’m supposed to care about.\n\nRobotics club too. I still care about the students, of course, and I know it matters, but the enjoyment part — the excitement I used to have — it’s just not really there. So I guess the answer is no, not much pleasure. Mostly I’m either canceling or going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you're moving, like feeling slowed down or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is really low. It’s not just being tired at the end of a normal workday, though I keep trying to tell myself that’s all it is. It feels heavier than that.\n\nLike, by the time I’ve gotten through teaching all day and then sat through a department meeting, my body feels so heavy. Even things that shouldn’t be a big deal — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, getting my bag ready for the next day — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “just for a minute” and then it’s like I can’t make myself get back up.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I’m restless exactly. It’s more slowed down, I guess. Like everything takes more effort than it should, and I’m constantly trying to push through this thick layer of exhaustion just to do basic things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like pants that used to fit normally are kind of hanging on me now, and I’ve had to tighten belts or just avoid certain things because they look strange.\n\nI’m not trying to lose weight. It’s more that on school days especially, I’ll realize it’s late afternoon and I haven’t really eaten anything. Sometimes I forget, or I know I should eat but I just don’t want to deal with it, so I put it off. Then by the time I notice, it’s like the whole day has gone by. So… yes, there’s been weight loss, or at least enough that I can see it in how my clothes fit, but it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had stressful stretches before — teaching kind of has seasons where everything feels like too much, and I’ve had times where I was exhausted or discouraged or just not myself for a bit. But I don’t know if I’d say it was like this.\n\nI’m trying to think back and not exaggerate or minimize it. There were times in college where I got overwhelmed, and other times during particularly rough school years where I’d sort of shut down for a weekend or feel low for a while. But I can’t clearly point to another period and say, yes, this was the same thing happening before. This feels… different, or at least more persistent, but I don’t know if that’s just because I’m in it right now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Not in a way I can confidently identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I’ve had times where I felt low before, or like I wasn’t really myself, but I’m not sure I’d describe them as the same kind of disconnected feeling.\n\nLike, there have been stressful periods where I wasn’t enjoying things much because I was overwhelmed or exhausted, but I can’t tell if that counts as a separate “period” in the way you mean. I’m trying not to over-answer or make it sound more definite than it is. This time feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s lasted and seeped into things, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t think I can confidently say yes, more than one period. I’m honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low and disconnected before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know I’ve said that a few times, but that really is the most honest answer I can give.\n\nI can remember feeling unhappy or overwhelmed before, definitely. I mean, I don’t think anyone gets to 33 without having some stretches where things feel heavy or where you’re sort of going through the motions. And with teaching, there have been periods where I felt worn down and not very connected to much outside of just getting through the week.\n\nBut when you say “this low and disconnected,” I hesitate, because I don’t know if I’ve had this exact thing before. I can’t point to another time in my life and say, yes, that was just like this. Maybe there were smaller versions of it, or times that looked similar from the outside, but I don’t know. This feels harder to shake, I guess, or more like it’s become part of the background.\n\nSo… maybe not? Or at least not in a way that I can clearly identify. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Is this the first time in your life you've felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I’ve had low times before, like I said, but this is the first time I can remember it affecting the things I usually actually look forward to in this way.\n\nLike softball — I used to really enjoy that. Even if I was tired after school, I’d usually feel better once I got there. Same with book club, or working in my garden, or helping with the robotics club. Those were things that felt like mine, or at least things that gave the week some shape.\n\nNow it’s more like I either cancel, or I show up and I’m just sort of… physically there. I go through the motions. I’ll sit at book club and listen, or I’ll go out to the garden and stare at what needs doing, but none of it really feels enjoyable. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something from them isn’t really connecting.\n\nSo if you mean this specific kind of low and disconnected from things I normally enjoy, then yes, I think this is the first time it’s felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before that resolved on its own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have a clear example of that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad and then eventually felt better, but I’m not sure I’d call those an “episode like this.” And I haven’t really had treatment for something like this before, at least not in a way where I could say, “Oh, that happened, and then it resolved because of treatment.”\n\nIt’s hard because I can look back and think, maybe there were periods where I was struggling more than I admitted at the time, and maybe I just pushed through because work kept going and life kept going. But I can’t point to a specific stretch that was this same kind of low and disconnected, and then say it clearly lifted on its own or with help.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is no, not that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low and disconnected in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not more than one in the way you’re asking.\n\nI keep turning it over because I don’t want to accidentally make it sound more certain than it is, but if I’m being honest, I can’t identify another separate time in my life where I felt this same low and this disconnected from things. I’ve had rough periods, definitely, and times where I was stressed or exhausted or not especially happy, but this feels different enough that I wouldn’t count those as the same thing.\n\nSo I’d say… no, not more than one episode that I can clearly point to. This is the only one I recognize as feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least, not in the way I used to.\n\nI keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to say “nothing” if that’s not completely fair, but the main things I used to actually look forward to just don’t feel good anymore. Softball used to be something I’d be excited about, even if I was tired — once I got there, I’d usually get into it. Now I either cancel or I go and I’m just kind of there, doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSame with book club. I used to like reading the book and hearing what people thought, even if I didn’t agree with them. Now I’ll sit there and listen, but it feels like I’m watching it happen from a distance. My garden is like that too. I know what needs to be done, and normally I’d enjoy being outside and getting my hands in the soil, but now I mostly just stare at it or make myself do little bits without really feeling anything from it.\n\nAnd robotics club — I used to enjoy helping the students problem-solve and seeing them get excited, but lately it feels more like another thing on the list. I don’t hate it. That’s the strange part. It’s not like I suddenly dislike all these things. It’s just that they don’t reach me the same way. So I guess I mostly go through the motions, or I avoid them, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this low and disconnected period you're experiencing now is related to any previous episodes of feeling low, or does it seem completely separate to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to sort of compare it to other times I felt low, because that seems like the natural thing to do, but I can’t quite make the pieces fit.\n\nThere have definitely been other hard patches in my life where I was unhappy or run-down or just not myself, but they felt more connected to whatever was happening at the time, if that makes sense. Like I could usually point to stress, or being overwhelmed, or just needing to get through something. This feels… harder to explain. Not completely separate, maybe, because it’s still me and my life, but I can’t clearly say, “Oh, this is the same thing coming back.”\n\nSo I guess I don’t know. It doesn’t feel like a repeat of a previous episode, because I’m not sure there really was one like this before. But I also don’t know that I can say it’s completely separate. It just feels different enough that I’m having trouble comparing it to anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is the first time you've experienced a low and disconnected period like this, or have you had something similar before?\n\nPatient: I think this is the first time it’s been like this.\n\nI’m hesitating because I don’t want to make it sound too neat, like there’s a clear line in my life where everything before was fine and now this is the only bad period I’ve ever had. That’s not true. I’ve had times when I felt low before, or worn out, or unhappy with how things were going. But when I think about whether I’ve had something similar to this specific feeling — this sort of low, disconnected, dulled-out feeling — I can’t really point to another time and say, “Yes, that was the same.”\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, yes, this is the first time I’ve experienced it like this. There may have been rough patches before, but not something I’d recognize as the same kind of thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t think so… not in a clear way.

I mean, there are little moments where I might seem a bit better, or I’ll get through a day and think, “Okay, that wasn’t as bad,” but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a proper period where I felt like myself again and then it came back. Softball used to be something I’d be excited about, even if I was tired — once I got there, I’d usually get into it. Now I either cancel or I go and I’m just kind of there, doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSame with book club. I used to like reading the book and hearing what people thought, even if I didn’t agree with them. Now I’ll sit there and listen, but it feels like I’m watching it happen from a distance. My garden is like that too. I know what needs to be done, and normally I’d enjoy being outside and getting my hands in the soil, but now I mostly just stare at it or make myself do little bits without really feeling anything from it.\n\nAnd robotics club — I used to enjoy helping the students problem-solve and seeing them get excited, but lately it feels more like another thing on the list. I don’t hate it. That’s the strange part. It’s not like I suddenly dislike all these things. It’s just that they don’t reach me the same way. So I guess I mostly go through the motions, or I avoid them, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this low and disconnected period you're experiencing now is related to any previous episodes of feeling low, or does it seem completely separate to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I’ve been trying to sort of compare it to other times I felt low, because that seems like the natural thing to do, but I can’t quite make the pieces fit.\n\nThere have definitely been other hard patches in my life where I was unhappy or run-down or just not myself, but they felt more connected to whatever was happening at the time, if that makes sense. Like I could usually point to stress, or being overwhelmed, or just needing to get through something. This feels… harder to explain. Not completely separate, maybe, because it’s still me and my life, but I can’t clearly say, “Oh, this is the same thing coming back.”\n\nSo I guess I don’t know. It doesn’t feel like a repeat of a previous episode, because I’m not sure there really was one like this before. But I also don’t know that I can say it’s completely separate. It just feels different enough that I’m having trouble comparing it to anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is the first time you've experienced a low and disconnected period like this, or have you had something similar before?\n\nPatient: I think this is the first time it’s been like this.\n\nI’m hesitating because I don’t want to make it sound too neat, like there’s a clear line in my life where everything before was fine and now this is the only bad period I’ve ever had. That’s not true. I’ve had times when I felt low before, or worn out, or unhappy with how things were going. But when I think about whether I’ve had something similar to this specific feeling — this sort of low, disconnected, dulled-out feeling — I can’t really point to another time and say, “Yes, that was the same.”\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, yes, this is the first time I’ve experienced it like this. There may have been rough patches before, but not something I’d recognize as the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past where you felt better for a while, and then this low and disconnected feeling came back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, there are little moments where I might seem a bit better, or I’ll get through a day and think, “Okay, that wasn’t as bad,” but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a proper period where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s more like the feeling shifts around a bit — some days it’s heavier, some days I can function a little more normally — but it doesn’t feel like it fully lifts.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to overstate it either way. There might have been brief stretches where I was distracted or busy and didn’t notice it as much, but when I look back, I don’t remember a real break from it. Not completely separate, maybe, because it’s still me and my life, but I can’t clearly say, “Oh, this is the same thing coming back.”\n\nSo I guess I don’t know. It doesn’t feel like a repeat of a previous episode, because I’m not sure there really was one like this before. But I also don’t know that I can say it’s completely separate. It just feels different enough that I’m having trouble comparing it to anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is the first time you've experienced a low and disconnected period like this, or have you had something similar before?\n\nPatient: I think this is the first time it’s been like this.\n\nI’m hesitating because I don’t want to make it sound too neat, like there’s a clear line in my life where everything before was fine and now this is the only bad period I’ve ever had. That’s not true. I’ve had times when I felt low before, or worn out, or unhappy with how things were going. But when I think about whether I’ve had something similar to this specific feeling — this sort of low, disconnected, dulled-out feeling — I can’t really point to another time and say, “Yes, that was the same.”\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, yes, this is the first time I’ve experienced it like this. There may have been rough patches before, but not something I’d recognize as the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past where you felt better for a while, and then this low and disconnected feeling came back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, there are little moments where I might seem a bit better, or I’ll get through a day and think, “Okay, that wasn’t as bad,” but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a proper period where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s more like the feeling shifts around a bit — some days it’s heavier, some days I can function a little more normally — but it doesn’t feel like it fully lifts.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to overstate it either way. There might have been brief stretches where I was distracted or busy and didn’t notice it as much, but when I look back, I don’t remember a real break from it. Not like, “Oh, I got better for a while, and then it returned.” It feels more continuous than that, just with some variation day to day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from the things you normally enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about 1 year and 7 months, since I took over the STEM assessment redesign on top of my regular teaching load.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. I’ve counted it backwards a few times because part of me keeps wondering if I’m exaggerating it, but it seems to line up pretty clearly with when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.

At the time, I told myself it was just because that was a lot to take on, and that once the initial pressure settled I’d feel more like myself again. But it didn’t really go back. It sort of became the point where I can look back and say, “That’s when this started,” even if I didn’t fully recognize it for what it was at the time.

So yes… roughly a year and seven months. Not just a bad week or a term that got away from me — it’s been going on since then.

So… yes, there’s been weight loss, or at least enough that I can see it in how my clothes fit, but it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. There were times in college where I got overwhelmed, and other times during particularly rough school years where I’d sort of shut down for a weekend or feel low for a while. But I can’t clearly point to another period and say, yes, this was the same thing happening before. This feels… different, or at least more persistent, but I don’t know if that’s just because I’m in it right now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. I’ve had times where I felt low before, or like I wasn’t really myself, but I’m not sure I’d describe them as the same kind of disconnected feeling.\n\nLike, there have been stressful periods where I wasn’t enjoying things much because I was overwhelmed or exhausted, but I can’t tell if that counts as a separate “period” in the way you mean. I’m trying not to over-answer or make it sound more definite than it is. This time feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s lasted and seeped into things, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t think I can confidently say yes, more than one period. I’m honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low and disconnected before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know I’ve said that a few times, but that really is the most honest answer I can give.\n\nI can remember feeling unhappy or overwhelmed before, definitely. I mean, I don’t think anyone gets to 33 without having some stretches where things feel heavy or where you’re sort of going through the motions. And with teaching, there have been periods where I felt worn down and not very connected to much outside of just getting through the week.\n\nBut when you say “this low and disconnected,” I hesitate, because I don’t know if I’ve had this exact thing before. I can’t point to another time in my life and say, yes, that was just like this. Maybe there were smaller versions of it, or times that looked similar from the outside, but I don’t know. This feels harder to shake, I guess, or more like it’s become part of the background.\n\nSo… maybe not? Or at least not in a way that I can clearly identify. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Is this the first time in your life you've felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I’ve had low times before, like I said, but this is the first time I can remember it affecting the things I usually actually look forward to in this way.\n\nLike softball — I used to really enjoy that. Even if I was tired after school, I’d usually feel better once I got there. Same with book club, or working in my garden, or helping with the robotics club. Those were things that felt like mine, or at least things that gave the week some shape.\n\nNow it’s more like I either cancel, or I show up and I’m just sort of… physically there. I go through the motions. I’ll sit at book club and listen, or I’ll go out to the garden and stare at what needs doing, but none of it really feels enjoyable. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. But I can’t point to a specific stretch that was this same kind of low and disconnected, and then say it clearly lifted on its own or with help.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is no, not that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low and disconnected in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’ve had rough periods, definitely, and times where I was stressed or exhausted or not especially happy, but this feels different enough that I wouldn’t count those as the same thing.\n\nSo I’d say… no, not more than one episode that I can clearly point to. Softball used to be something I’d be excited about, even if I was tired — once I got there, I’d usually get into it. Now I either cancel or I go and I’m just kind of there, doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSame with book club. I used to like reading the book and hearing what people thought, even if I didn’t agree with them. Now I’ll sit there and listen, but it feels like I’m watching it happen from a distance. My garden is like that too. Even things that shouldn’t be a big deal — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, getting my bag ready for the next day — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “just for a minute” and then it’s like I can’t make myself get back up.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I’m restless exactly. It’s more slowed down, I guess. Like everything takes more effort than it should, and I’m constantly trying to push through this thick layer of exhaustion just to do basic things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like pants that used to fit normally are kind of hanging on me now, and I’ve had to tighten belts or just avoid certain things because they look strange.\n\nI’m not trying to lose weight. It’s more that on school days especially, I’ll realize it’s late afternoon and I haven’t really eaten anything. Sometimes I forget, or I know I should eat but I just don’t want to deal with it, so I put it off. Then by the time I notice, it’s like the whole day has gone by. So… yes, there’s been weight loss, or at least enough that I can see it in how my clothes fit, but it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had stressful stretches before — teaching kind of has seasons where everything feels like too much, and I’ve had times where I was exhausted or discouraged or just not myself for a bit. But I don’t know if I’d say it was like this.\n\nI’m trying to think back and not exaggerate or minimize it. There were times in college where I got overwhelmed, and other times during particularly rough school years where I’d sort of shut down for a weekend or feel low for a while. But I can’t clearly point to another period and say, yes, this was the same thing happening before. This feels… different, or at least more persistent, but I don’t know if that’s just because I’m in it right now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Not in a way I can confidently identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I’ve had times where I felt low before, or like I wasn’t really myself, but I’m not sure I’d describe them as the same kind of disconnected feeling.\n\nLike, there have been stressful periods where I wasn’t enjoying things much because I was overwhelmed or exhausted, but I can’t tell if that counts as a separate “period” in the way you mean. I’m trying not to over-answer or make it sound more definite than it is. This time feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s lasted and seeped into things, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t think I can confidently say yes, more than one period. I’m honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low and disconnected before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know I’ve said that a few times, but that really is the most honest answer I can give.\n\nI can remember feeling unhappy or overwhelmed before, definitely. I mean, I don’t think anyone gets to 33 without having some stretches where things feel heavy or where you’re sort of going through the motions. And with teaching, there have been periods where I felt worn down and not very connected to much outside of just getting through the week.\n\nBut when you say “this low and disconnected,” I hesitate, because I don’t know if I’ve had this exact thing before. I can’t point to another time in my life and say, yes, that was just like this. Maybe there were smaller versions of it, or times that looked similar from the outside, but I don’t know. This feels harder to shake, I guess, or more like it’s become part of the background.\n\nSo… maybe not? Or at least not in a way that I can clearly identify. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Is this the first time in your life you've felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I’ve had low times before, like I said, but this is the first time I can remember it affecting the things I usually actually look forward to in this way.\n\nLike softball — I used to really enjoy that. Even if I was tired after school, I’d usually feel better once I got there. Same with book club, or working in my garden, or helping with the robotics club. Those were things that felt like mine, or at least things that gave the week some shape.\n\nNow it’s more like I either cancel, or I show up and I’m just sort of… physically there. I go through the motions. I’ll sit at book club and listen, or I’ll go out to the garden and stare at what needs doing, but none of it really feels enjoyable. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something from them isn’t really connecting.\n\nSo if you mean this specific kind of low and disconnected from things I normally enjoy, then yes, I think this is the first time it’s felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before that resolved on its own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have a clear example of that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad and then eventually felt better, but I’m not sure I’d call those an “episode like this.” And I haven’t really had treatment for something like this before, at least not in a way where I could say, “Oh, that happened, and then it resolved because of treatment.”\n\nIt’s hard because I can look back and think, maybe there were periods where I was struggling more than I admitted at the time, and maybe I just pushed through because work kept going and life kept going. But I can’t point to a specific stretch that was this same kind of low and disconnected, and then say it clearly lifted on its own or with help.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is no, not that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low and disconnected in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not more than one in the way you’re asking.\n\nI keep turning it over because I don’t want to accidentally make it sound more certain than it is, but if I’m being honest, I can’t identify another separate time in my life where I felt this same low and this disconnected from things. I’ve had rough periods, definitely, and times where I was stressed or exhausted or not especially happy, but this feels different enough that I wouldn’t count those as the same thing.\n\nSo I’d say… no, not more than one episode that I can clearly point to. This is the only one I recognize as feeling like this.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Not really… or at least, not in the way I used to.\n\nI keep trying to think of something, because I don’t want to say “nothing” if that’s not completely fair, but the main things I used to actually look forward to just don’t feel good anymore. Softball used to be something I’d be excited about, even if I was tired — once I got there, I’d usually get into it. Now I either cancel or I go and I’m just kind of there, doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nSame with book club. I used to like reading the book and hearing what people thought, even if I didn’t agree with them. Now I’ll sit there and listen, but it feels like I’m watching it happen from a distance. My garden is like that too. I know what needs to be done, and normally I’d enjoy being outside and getting my hands in the soil, but now I mostly just stare at it or make myself do little bits without really feeling anything from it.\n\nAnd robotics club — I used to enjoy helping the students problem-solve and seeing them get excited, but lately it feels more like another thing on the list. I don’t hate it. That’s the strange part. It’s not like I suddenly dislike all these things. It’s just that they don’t reach me the same way. So I guess I mostly go through the motions, or I avoid them, because none of it really feels enjoyable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this low and disconnected period you're experiencing now is related to any previous episodes of feeling low, or does it seem completely separate to you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. Like I could usually point to stress, or being overwhelmed, or just needing to get through something. This feels… harder to explain. Not completely separate, maybe, because it’s still me and my life, but I can’t clearly say, “Oh, this is the same thing coming back.”\n\nSo I guess I don’t know. It just feels different enough that I’m having trouble comparing it to anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this is the first time you've experienced a low and disconnected period like this, or have you had something similar before?\n\nPatient: I think this is the first time it’s been like this.\n\nI’m hesitating because I don’t want to make it sound too neat, like there’s a clear line in my life where everything before was fine and now this is the only bad period I’ve ever had. But when I think about whether I’ve had something similar to this specific feeling — this sort of low, disconnected, dulled-out feeling — I can’t really point to another time and say, “Yes, that was the same.”\n\nSo… as far as I can tell, yes, this is the first time I’ve experienced it like this. There might have been brief stretches where I was distracted or busy and didn’t notice it as much, but when I look back, I don’t remember a real break from it. Not like, “Oh, I got better for a while, and then it returned.” It feels more continuous than that, just with some variation day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from the things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. There may have been rough patches before, but not something I’d recognize as the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past where you felt better for a while, and then this low and disconnected feeling came back?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, there are little moments where I might seem a bit better, or I’ll get through a day and think, “Okay, that wasn’t as bad,” but I wouldn’t say I’ve had a proper period where I felt like myself again and then it came back. It’s more like the feeling shifts around a bit — some days it’s heavier, some days I can function a little more normally — but it doesn’t feel like it fully lifts.\n\nI’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to overstate it either way. There might have been brief stretches where I was distracted or busy and didn’t notice it as much, but when I look back, I don’t remember a real break from it. Not like, “Oh, I got better for a while, and then it returned.” It feels more continuous than that, just with some variation day to day.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this low and disconnected from the things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a year and seven months now, I think. I’ve counted it backwards a few times because part of me keeps wondering if I’m exaggerating it, but it seems to line up pretty clearly with when I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nAt the time, I told myself it was just because that was a lot to take on, and that once the initial pressure settled I’d feel more like myself again. But it didn’t really go back. It sort of became the point where I can look back and say, “That’s when this started,” even if I didn’t fully recognize it for what it was at the time.\n\nSo yes… roughly a year and seven months. Not just a bad week or a term that got away from me — it’s been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode of feeling this low and disconnected before that went away and then came back?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so — not in the way you mean.\n\nI know I’ve sort of answered this already, but I’m trying to be accurate. I don’t remember having a separate episode before where I felt like this, then it properly went away, and then later came back. There have been ups and downs within this period, definitely, like some days I can push through more or I feel a bit less numb, but I wouldn’t call those “it went away.”\n\nIt’s more that this has felt like one long stretch with variations in intensity, rather than separate episodes. I can’t really point to an earlier time in my life and say, “That was the same thing, and then I recovered from it.” So… no, as far as I can tell, this hasn’t happened before in that pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression like this one before?\n\nPatient: Not that I can identify, no.\n\nI’m sorry, I know I keep circling around the same answer, but I’m trying to be really honest rather than just saying a quick yes or no. I don’t remember another time before this where it was like this — where I felt this low and disconnected for a stretch and then came out of it. I’ve had stressful periods, obviously, and times where I was tired or unhappy or overwhelmed, but I wouldn’t put them in the same category as this.\n\nSo as far as I can tell, this is the first time it’s been like this. I can’t point to a previous episode and say, “Yes, that happened before.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And sometimes, especially on the drive home, my mind goes to this place where I wonder if my family, or even my students, would somehow be better off without me around.\n\nI don’t want to act on it — I really don’t — and that’s part of why I came in, because the fact that those thoughts are showing up at all is frightening. I’m a teacher, I’m used to being responsible and holding things together for other people, and lately these thoughts have felt… too heavy to just keep brushing aside.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these thoughts about not wanting to wake up or wondering if others would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… longer than I’d like to admit. About a year and seven months, I think. It started around the time I took over the STEM assessment redesign at school, on top of my normal teaching load.\n\nAt first I don’t think I even recognized them as those kinds of thoughts. It was more like, “I’m so exhausted, I wish I could just disappear for a while,” or, “I don’t want to wake up and have to do all of this again.” But over time it became more direct and harder to ignore. And because that project just kept stretching on, and I still had my regular classes and responsibilities, it feels like those thoughts have been kind of… following me since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely. That’s actually been one of the things that made me realize something was really wrong, because I used to be the kind of person who had things I genuinely looked forward to.\n\nI used to play softball, and even if I was tired from school, once I got there I’d usually feel better — being outside, joking with people, just focusing on the game for a while. Now I either cancel or I show up and it’s like I’m physically there but not really… enjoying any of it. I’m just counting down until I can go home.\n\nSame with book club. I used to love reading and talking about the book, even when we got completely off topic. Lately I either haven’t finished the book or I go and just sit there, nodding, trying to seem normal. My garden too — that used to be calming for me, checking on things after work, planting, weeding, all of that. Now it feels like another chore I’m failing at.\n\nAnd robotics club at school… that one makes me feel especially guilty, because the students are excited and I know it matters to them. I still try to be there, but I don’t feel that spark I used to feel. I’m mostly going through the motions. It’s like the things that used to make me feel like myself don’t really reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience pleasure when you're doing these activities, even if you're not looking forward to them as much?\n\nPatient: Not really… no. I keep hoping that maybe if I just get myself there, the feeling will come back once I’m in it, because that used to happen. Like with softball, even if I’d had a long day, once we started playing I could usually get caught up in it and laugh or feel competitive or just enjoy being outside. Now I can be standing there on the field and it’s like I’m watching myself participate. I know it’s something I used to love, but I don’t actually feel much from it.\n\nBook club is similar. I’ll go sometimes because I don’t want people to notice I’m pulling away, but I’m mostly just sitting there, trying to follow along and seem interested. The conversation that used to feel fun just feels like noise I have to respond to.\n\nAnd my garden… I used to get this quiet satisfaction from it, like checking on the plants after work felt grounding. Now even when I do it, it doesn’t feel peaceful. It just feels like another thing I’m supposed to care about.\n\nRobotics club too. I still care about the students, of course, and I know it matters, but the enjoyment part — the excitement I used to have — it’s just not really there. So I guess the answer is no, not much pleasure. Mostly I’m either canceling or going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or how you're moving, like feeling slowed down or restless?\n\nPatient: Yes… my energy is really low. It’s not just being tired at the end of a normal workday, though I keep trying to tell myself that’s all it is. It feels heavier than that.\n\nLike, by the time I’ve gotten through teaching all day and then sat through a department meeting, my body feels so heavy. Even things that shouldn’t be a big deal — making dinner, answering a few parent emails, getting my bag ready for the next day — they feel like climbing a hill. I’ll sit down “just for a minute” and then it’s like I can’t make myself get back up.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say I’m restless exactly. It’s more slowed down, I guess. Like everything takes more effort than it should, and I’m constantly trying to push through this thick layer of exhaustion just to do basic things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes have definitely gotten looser over the past year. Like pants that used to fit normally are kind of hanging on me now, and I’ve had to tighten belts or just avoid certain things because they look strange.\n\nI’m not trying to lose weight. It’s more that on school days especially, I’ll realize it’s late afternoon and I haven’t really eaten anything. Sometimes I forget, or I know I should eat but I just don’t want to deal with it, so I put it off. Then by the time I notice, it’s like the whole day has gone by. So… yes, there’s been weight loss, or at least enough that I can see it in how my clothes fit, but it wasn’t intentional.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced periods like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had stressful stretches before — teaching kind of has seasons where everything feels like too much, and I’ve had times where I was exhausted or discouraged or just not myself for a bit. But I don’t know if I’d say it was like this.\n\nI’m trying to think back and not exaggerate or minimize it. There were times in college where I got overwhelmed, and other times during particularly rough school years where I’d sort of shut down for a weekend or feel low for a while. But I can’t clearly point to another period and say, yes, this was the same thing happening before. This feels… different, or at least more persistent, but I don’t know if that’s just because I’m in it right now.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I don’t know. Not in a way I can confidently identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I’ve had times where I felt low before, or like I wasn’t really myself, but I’m not sure I’d describe them as the same kind of disconnected feeling.\n\nLike, there have been stressful periods where I wasn’t enjoying things much because I was overwhelmed or exhausted, but I can’t tell if that counts as a separate “period” in the way you mean. I’m trying not to over-answer or make it sound more definite than it is. This time feels more noticeable to me, more… like it’s lasted and seeped into things, I guess.\n\nSo I don’t think I can confidently say yes, more than one period. I’m honestly not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low and disconnected before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I know I’ve said that a few times, but that really is the most honest answer I can give.\n\nI can remember feeling unhappy or overwhelmed before, definitely. I mean, I don’t think anyone gets to 33 without having some stretches where things feel heavy or where you’re sort of going through the motions. And with teaching, there have been periods where I felt worn down and not very connected to much outside of just getting through the week.\n\nBut when you say “this low and disconnected,” I hesitate, because I don’t know if I’ve had this exact thing before. I can’t point to another time in my life and say, yes, that was just like this. Maybe there were smaller versions of it, or times that looked similar from the outside, but I don’t know. This feels harder to shake, I guess, or more like it’s become part of the background.\n\nSo… maybe not? Or at least not in a way that I can clearly identify. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: Is this the first time in your life you've felt this low and disconnected from things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, I’ve had low times before, like I said, but this is the first time I can remember it affecting the things I usually actually look forward to in this way.\n\nLike softball — I used to really enjoy that. Even if I was tired after school, I’d usually feel better once I got there. Same with book club, or working in my garden, or helping with the robotics club. Those were things that felt like mine, or at least things that gave the week some shape.\n\nNow it’s more like I either cancel, or I show up and I’m just sort of… physically there. I go through the motions. I’ll sit at book club and listen, or I’ll go out to the garden and stare at what needs doing, but none of it really feels enjoyable. It’s not that I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like the part of me that used to get something from them isn’t really connecting.\n\nSo if you mean this specific kind of low and disconnected from things I normally enjoy, then yes, I think this is the first time it’s felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before that resolved on its own or with treatment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I have a clear example of that, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where I felt bad and then eventually felt better, but I’m not sure I’d call those an “episode like this.” And I haven’t really had treatment for something like this before, at least not in a way where I could say, “Oh, that happened, and then it resolved because of treatment.”\n\nIt’s hard because I can look back and think, maybe there were periods where I was struggling more than I admitted at the time, and maybe I just pushed through because work kept going and life kept going. But I can’t point to a specific stretch that was this same kind of low and disconnected, and then say it clearly lifted on its own or with help.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is no, not that I can identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one episode of feeling this low and disconnected in your life?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not more than one in the way you’re asking.\n\nI keep turning it over because I don’t want to accidentally make it sound more certain than it is, but if I’m being honest, I can’t identify another separate time in my life where I felt this same low and this disconnected from things. I’ve had rough periods, definitely, and times where I was stressed or exhausted or not especially happy, but this feels different enough that I wouldn’t count those as the same thing.\n\nSo I’d say… no, not more than one episode that I can clearly point to.
